❯ /model                                                                        
  ⎿  Set model to Opus 4.7 (1M context) (default)

❯ Please read the PRD and DESIGN docs in this directory. I want you to review   
  the plan and then interview me to validate that it's the right plan. Also,    
  I'm going to want interactive mocks to validate key user journeys and UI.     

  Read 2 files, listed 2 directories (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I've read both docs. Before any mocks, I want to pressure-test a few areas
  where the plan is underspecified or internally inconsistent. Better to surface
   this now than draw screens against a moving target.

  Inconsistencies inside the docs:
  - PRD §3 lists recurring_cashflow as a primitive; DESIGN §3.2's ActionType
  omits it.
  - DESIGN §6's NUA flow uses a tax_deduction action that isn't in ActionType
  either.
  - PRD §4.3 talks about an effective_state_tax variable; DESIGN's ActorState
  only has a single effective_tax_rate.

  Round 1 — scope & engine semantics (these will reshape the UI, so I want to 
  settle them first):

  1. Who's the user and what question are they trying to answer? "Can I retire
  at X?", "Will I run out of money?", "How much can I spend safely?", or "What's
   my terminal net worth?" The driving question reshapes the chart, the events
  surfaced, and what "success" looks like.
  2. Single actor or couple? Couples need two ages, two SS streams, staggered
  RMDs, survivor mechanics. If V1 is single-actor by design, fine — just want it
   explicit.
  3. Recurring income/expense model. How do salary, retirement spending, Social
  Security, pensions, and RMDs fit? Are they N discrete events on the timeline
  (verbose) or a separate recurring_cashflow concept the engine ticks every
  year? PRD hints at the latter; DESIGN doesn't model it.
  4. Withdrawal sequencing. When annual spending exceeds income, where does cash
   come from? User-defined order (taxable → tax-deferred → Roth), pro-rata, or
  smart/tax-optimized? This is usually the heart of a retirement model and is
  currently absent.
  5. Single vs multi-asset returns. Environment.equity_return is one number.
  Real portfolios are stocks/bonds/cash with different return profiles. Do you
  want one blended return per account, per-account asset allocation, or a
  multi-track environment (equity/bond/cash)?
  6. The "cone" math. ±volatility applied independently each year compounds to
  an absurdly wide cone over 30+ years. Most planners hold "what if returns are
  X% lower for the whole horizon" rather than independent annual draws. Which do
   you intend?

  I have follow-ups queued on NUA edge cases, scenario comparison/save-load,
  real-vs-nominal UX, and what the timeline editor actually looks like — but
  answers above will steer those. Once you've responded, I'll propose a concrete
   mock plan (probably 3–4 key screens/journeys) before drawing anything.

✻ Churned for 1m 5s

※ recap: Goal: validate the plan for a local-first event-driven personal finance modeler before building interactive mocks. I posed six scope questions (user intent, single vs couple, recurring cashflows, withdrawal sequencing, multi-asset returns, cone math) and flagged doc inconsistencies. Next: await your answers. ❯ /help 
  ⎿  Help dialog dismissed

❯ 1- the primary user is someone who *can* retire but has not yet done so. They 
   aren't focused on long term accumulation for retirement so much as wealth    
  preservation, tax planning, and even inheritance planning. A key part of that 
   is modeling decisions about when and how much to sell, how to diversify,     
  etc. I do feel that this tool would be useful to a broad range of users who   
  want to model financial hypotheticals including tax situations.               

⏺ That clarification reshapes the problem. Decumulation + preservation + tax +
  inheritance is a fundamentally different beast from accumulation, and it
  exposes a real tension in the existing docs:

  Biggest tension: PRD §4.3 commits V1 to blended effective tax rates, but tax
  planning is your user's primary job. The interesting decisions — Roth
  conversions, capital-gains harvesting, NUA, IRMAA cliffs, ordinary-vs-LTCG
  bracket arbitrage, "should I realize gains this year or next?" — all hinge on
  bracket math, not blended rates. A single effective_tax_rate literally cannot
  answer "should I convert $50k to Roth this year?" because the answer depends
  on where that $50k lands in the bracket stack. I think this assumption needs
  to be revisited or the tool won't earn its keep with its target user.

  Round 2:

  1. Tax fidelity. Given the persona, I'd argue V1 needs at minimum: separate
  ordinary-income and LTCG/qualified-div brackets with configurable tables, plus
   probably IRMAA tiers. Are you willing to upgrade from blended rates, or is
  there a reason to keep V1 simple?
  2. Step-up basis at death. Central to inheritance planning. Should the engine
  model terminal step-up (cost_basis → balance at death age) for taxable
  accounts, and is "death age" / "horizon age" an explicit input?
  3. Withdrawal sequencing & Roth conversions.
    - When spending exceeds income, who decides which account to draw from — a
  user-defined ordered policy, or explicit per-year liquidate events?
    - Are Roth conversions a first-class macro (ordinary income on conversion,
  tax-free growth after)?
  4. Multi-asset returns. Since "diversify" is a primary lever, does each
  account carry an allocation (e.g., 60/40), and does Environment expose
  multiple return tracks (equity/bond/cash)? Or stay one-knob and let users
  blend themselves?
  5. Couple modeling. Inheritance/estate planning usually involves a spouse —
  two ages, two SS streams, survivor scenarios. V1 single-actor or in scope?

  Pin these down and I'll propose a concrete mock plan — likely 4 screens
  covering: environment editor, timeline+chart, an event composer with NUA &
  Roth-conversion as worked examples, and scenario compare. The answers above
  dictate the controls those screens need.

✻ Crunched for 47s

❯ 1- I agree. One of the "actions" I imagined was a move to a more tax friendly 
   locale (country or state)                                                    
  2- This is interesting but unless we can model this as a kind of event /      
  action / transaction I would defer this to a future milestone.                
  3- This is a good question to which I don't have a well informed answer.      
  Ideally the user can work either at the portfolio level where withdrawals are 
   distributed across accounts or at the account level where the user           
  explicitly chooses which account is in play. Maybe this is a case for a macro 
   where the user can peer in to tweak. I don't know how to answer your second  
  part about Roth conversions.                                                  
  4- I definitely want to track assets explicitly within accounts. I wasn't     
  thinking about a magic "diversify" lever. I wonder if this and other things   
  can be modeled as groupings, macros, or even helper UI                        
  5- That should be a configuration choice or treat marriage (and I suppose     
  divorce) as another kind of event. For now and simplicity we can assume       
  married couple filing jointly.                                                

⏺ Good — that's enough to land recommendations, surface what's still open, and
  propose mocks.

  Recommendations (push back on any of these):

  1. Tax engine. Bracket-based federal + state tables. Locale move = swap state
  table. Brackets for ordinary income and LTCG/qualified dividends; IRMAA tiers
  optional. User-editable so non-US plug-ins work.
  2. Assets within accounts. Account becomes a tax-treatment container; each
  account holds Holdings[] with asset_class (equity/bond/cash/other), balance,
  cost_basis. Engine applies returns per-asset-class. Environment exposes
  multiple return tracks. Diversification then is a real lever — no magic.
  3. Withdrawal sequencing. Two layers: (a) spending_policy on actor state with
  an ordered withdrawal rule (e.g., taxable → tax-deferred → Roth) that the
  engine auto-applies when cash needs > income; (b) explicit liquidate
  primitives override for any year. The "macro you can peer into" pattern is
  right — call it an "Annual Withdrawal" macro.
  4. Roth conversions. First-class macro. Move $X Traditional → Roth, taxed as
  ordinary income that year, tax-free thereafter. Common variant is an annual
  ladder macro across an age range. Top-3 lever for your persona.
  5. Step-up at death. I'd push back on deferring. As a primitive it's one line
  — set cost_basis = balance on taxable accounts at horizon age. Without it,
  terminal net-worth-to-heirs silently overstates tax cost of holding
  appreciated assets. Keep the primitive in V1; defer the inheritance-specific
  UI.

  Still open before I draw anything:

  - Cone math. Independent annual ±volatility (compounds absurdly wide) vs
  single-shock-for-whole-horizon? I lean toward the latter — it's what a
  preservation-minded user actually worries about.
  - Recurring cashflows. I'd add a recurring_cashflow primitive (start_age,
  end_age, amount, inflation-adjusted?, source/destination account). Resolves
  the PRD/DESIGN mismatch and covers salary, SS, pension, RMDs, baseline
  spending in one shape.
  - Scenario compare. For a tax-planning tool, overlaying two scenarios on one
  chart is arguably the feature. In scope for V1?

  Mock plan — 4 screens:

  1. Setup / Actor state — accounts + holdings + cost basis editor. Validates
  data-entry burden (your biggest UX risk for a serious user).
  2. Main view: timeline + chart — env sliders, event chips on age axis,
  projection with cone, real/nominal toggle, hover-to-inspect. The hero screen.
  3. Event composer — add/edit a macro, with NUA, Roth-conversion ladder, locale
   move, and recurring withdrawal as worked examples. Validates the "macro with
  peerable primitives" pattern.
  4. Scenario compare — two scenarios overlaid; diff'd in $ and tax-paid terms.

  Meta-question on mocks: the value prop is "instant feedback as you drag
  sliders." Static Figma-style screens won't validate that feel. I'd build these
   as live React + Vite with seeded fake data and a stub engine — clickable,
  real sliders, real chart re-render — but no persistence and no real tax tables
   yet. OK?

  If yes, I'd start with #2 (main view) since it shows whether the cone + event
  chips + sliders actually feel right. Want me to start there?

⏺ Server's up at http://localhost:5173/ and Vite is transforming all the source
  files cleanly. tsc reports no errors.

  I should be honest about one limit: I can't open it in a browser to confirm
  the components actually render correctly. The HTTP 200s + clean tsc are
  necessary but not sufficient — there could still be a runtime error inside
  React. Open the URL and let me know what you see.

  What's in this mock:

  - Top bar — Nominal $ / Today's $ toggle (real/nominal switches the whole
  chart)
  - Left panel — sliders for inflation, equity/bond/cash returns, volatility,
  plus actor sliders for spending, income, tax rate, horizon age. All re-render
  the chart live.
  - Summary strip — 4 stats: today's net worth, terminal net worth + cone range,
   lifetime taxes, worst-case ruin age
  - Chart — baseline line, dashed best/worst envelopes, shaded cone band,
  vertical reference lines for each event age. Hovering an event highlights its
  line.
  - Timeline + event list — chips on an age axis (color-coded: gold=one-shot,
  green=recurring, purple=macro). The Roth ladder shows as a span. Each event
  row has a draggable age slider and shows its primitive actions as code chips.

  Seeded scenario: age-62 pre-retiree, MFJ, $3.1M across taxable / Traditional
  401k / Roth / cash with cost basis. Events: Retire@67, NUA@64, Roth ladder
  65–72, FL move + SS@70.

  Engine caveats (fair to know what's real vs theatre):
  - Per-asset growth, tax drag on taxable equity/bond, blended-rate withdrawal
  sequencing (taxable → tax-deferred → Roth), inflation-adjusted spending — all
  working.
  - liquidate_asset/purchase_asset/tax_deduction primitives are no-ops in this
  mock — the NUA chip is visually correct but doesn't yet move money. Same for
  recurring_cashflow end-dates (it just keeps depositing).
  - Cone is horizon-shock as we discussed: best = baseline + vol applied each
  year, worst = baseline – vol. Compounds smoothly.

  Things to assess as you play with it:

  1. Complexity feel — too much on screen? Right amount? Sliders panel
  reasonable, or already feeling cramped?
  2. Cone legibility — does the dashed envelope + shaded band communicate
  "uncertainty" without being noisy?
  3. Event chips on the age axis — is overlap a problem when events cluster?
  (Try moving Roth-ladder start with the event slider — chips will pile up.)
  4. Real-vs-Nominal toggle — does the magnitude shift make intuitive sense?
  5. The summary strip stats — are these the four numbers that matter, or do you
   want different ones (success-prob style? terminal-to-heirs?)?

  Tell me what feels off and I'll iterate, or move to mock #3 (event composer
  with NUA / Roth-ladder as worked examples) once you've poked at this.

※ recap: We're validating the financial-modeler PRD/DESIGN by building interactive React mocks; mock #2 (timeline + chart main view) is running at http://localhost:5173/. Open it and tell me how the complexity feels. (disable recaps in /config)

❯ The mock worked quite well but had a few problems, big and small.             
                                                                                
  The UI for moving events around the timeline is very awkward. I think it      
  would make more sense to show them as draggable nodes on the primary timeline 
   view. Trying to move the slider on each event in the list was painful as the 
   event would reorder itself in the list and break the drag.                   
                                                                                
  The view shows some things as globals but I'm not sure anything really is     
  global. They're just starting conditions (e.g. current rate of inflation or   
  average bond yield) that should be changeable on the timeline. That's the     
  whole point of the tool is to explore various what-if scenarios around        
  environmental (e.g. inflation, tax brackets), personal (where I live), and    
  per-asset transactions (e.g. NUA or acquiring a new house).                   
                                                                                
  I've always thought about this tool as an accounting-like ledger. Only        
  instead of tracking past transactions (which it might also do), it helps the  
  user model future transactions and explore how changing the variables of      
  those transactions affects the long term picture.                             
                                                                                
  So I see the app as a set of accounts with values (e.g. start date, start     
  value, yield) and events that change those values. For convenience it would   
  be nice if events could be global (economic downturn affecting all assets of  
  a certain type) or account (sale of assets on a particular account). Jobs,    
  Social Security, Expenses are all just accounts that produce or consume       
  money. The NUA case is special. It literally only affects one asset once. A   
  reasonable UX might simply have the user explicitly "attach" an event to      
  multiple accounts and then describe the variable changes it represents. For   
  example, a "sale" event attached to a house would create an end-date for the  
  asset, produce cash, and incur capital gains taxes. A "move" event would      
  change the tax rates. Hmmm, I wonder if tax rates and other "global" like     
  things should probably be environmental or "inherited" values that are used   
  by child accounts.                                                            

⏺ Big reframe — and I think you're right that it simplifies rather than
  complicates. Let me reflect it back, lock a few decisions before I rebuild,
  then propose v2.

  The unified mental model (what I heard):

  - Everything is an account. Assets, liabilities, income streams, expense
  streams, and ambient things (inflation, equity-return, tax-jurisdiction). Each
   has a starting value and a trajectory.
  - Events are transformations placed on the timeline. Each event attaches to ≥1
   accounts and mutates their values at that age. Scope = whatever it's attached
   to. "Global" is just "attached to an ambient account."
  - Sliders aren't globals — they're starting values for ambient accounts.
  "Inflation 3%" is the inflation account's value at age=now. Change it over
  time by placing events.
  - Inheritance. Some accounts read from others — a stock holding's growth
  references the equity-return account; the actor's effective tax rate
  references the jurisdiction account. "Move to FL" = an event that swaps the
  jurisdiction reference.
  - NUA-style specificity. Holdings are first-class so events can target "the
  company-stock lot in my 401k," not just the account.

  Four decisions I want locked before rebuilding:

  1. Holding-level identity. NUA implies holdings are first-class with id/name
  (e.g., "AAPL ESPP lot"), so events can target one holding. Agreed?
  2. Income/expense as accounts. "Job" = account that produces $X/yr until age
  67; "spending" = account that consumes $Y/yr inflation-adjusted. "Retire" =
  event that end-dates the job. Cleaner model, but the user must explicitly
  create these. Worth the extra setup vs scalar fields?
  3. Inheritance — explicit or by-type?
    - Explicit: each holding declares "my growth = equity_return" by reference.
    - By-type: equity holdings auto-bind to equity-return; bonds to bond-return;
   etc.

  I lean by-type with optional override. Your call.
  4. Multi-attach semantics. When one event hits multiple accounts, are
  parameters shared (one $ amount applied to all) or per-attachment (each
  account gets its own)? "Market crash —30% to equity" is shared; "move to FL"
  needs per-account. Likely need both: event has parameters, each attachment
  maps them.

  Proposed v2:

  - Drag events on the chart's age axis directly. Single-event nodes drag
  horizontally; ranged events get start/end handles. Click a node to edit. Kill
  the slider-in-a-list pattern entirely.
  - Replace left panel with an Accounts tree. Three groups: Personal (taxable,
  401k, house), Ambient (inflation, equity-return, jurisdiction, tax-brackets),
  Streams (job, spending, SS). Click to edit start value + trajectory. Sliders
  move into the per-account editor.
  - Event editor (right side or modal) opens on node click — pick attached
  accounts, configure parameters per attachment.

  This is a meaningful rebuild (a few hundred lines) but the unified data model
  makes later mocks much cleaner.

  Saving the mental model + the slider-in-list lesson to memory now. Want me to
  proceed with v2 once you've answered the four decisions, or pin down anything
  else first?

  Wrote 3 memories (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Saved. Awaiting your answers on the four decisions before I rebuild.

✻ Cooked for 1m 57s

❯ 1- yes, I don't think of accounts as "my Schwab account" or my "Personal      
  bank." I think of accounts as "my MSFT holdings at Schwab" or possibly a      
  Schwab account that contains an MSFT account.                                 
  2- yes, although I see a convenience helper where for a given income producer 
   (e.g. a contract) I can enter the end-date and that automatically creates    
  the "end this income account" event.                                          
  3- I don't know. Your proposal to do by-type with override sounds fine. It    
  might also be convenient to tie this to the accounts tree. Then I could       
  create US Economy as a parent and set yields and inflations there. It would   
  also be relatively easy to express some account values as modifiers of the    
  parent values. Maybe there's an explicit syntax we can use: yield+2%          
  4- I think parameters are shared. If we want to get very clever we could      
  create an event tree as well. A global economic downturn might play out       
  differently in different regions. For now let's assume shared parameters and  
  let the user create multiple events if they really want to have different     
  parameters apply to different accounts.                                       
                                                                                
  The goal of the v2 mock should be to validate the model's utility for a user  
  to explore the impact of future decisions with enough real world complexity   
  to make it useful. I don't know if that helps.                                

⏺ That helps a lot — the validation goal is exactly what I needed. It tells me
  to optimize the seed scenario for realism over coverage (one rich, plausible
  household, not 12 toy edge cases) and to make sure the user can poke at it and
   see consequences propagate. If you can't believably model your own situation
  in this mock, it fails the test.

  A few things crystallized from your answers:

  - Accounts are a tree, not a list. Schwab > MSFT lot. US Economy >
  equity_yield. Tax > California vs Florida (states as siblings under a Tax
  parent). This unifies "ambient" and "personal" — both are just nodes in the
  same tree at different scopes. Inheritance follows the parent chain.
  - Override syntax (yield + 2%): for the mock I'll skip the parser and use a
  simpler override: { mode: 'absolute' | 'delta', value: 0.02 } shape per field.
   Same concept, no parsing. We can upgrade to expressions later.
  - Convenience helpers (end_date on a contract auto-spawning the end-event):
  I'll hardcode the resulting event in seed for v2 and mark it as "would be
  auto-generated." That validates the outcome without building the helper
  plumbing yet.
  - Shared parameters + no event tree. Got it. Multi-attach events apply the
  same parameters uniformly to all attached accounts.

  v2 plan:

  1. Schema rewrite: Account becomes a tree node with kind (asset / liability /
  income / expense / category / ambient), parent_id, children, and a values slot
   (start_value, yield, tax_treatment, etc.) that resolves via parent chain.
  Holdings are leaf accounts under custodians. Events attach to N accounts,
  carry parameters, fire by age.
  2. Seed: US Economy ambient parent (yields + inflation); Tax parent with
  California & Florida children; Personal subtree with Schwab → {MSFT lot, VTI
  lot, bond fund}, Fidelity 401(k) → {company-stock lot, index funds}, Vanguard
  Roth IRA, Cash, House, Salary (income), Annual spending (expense), Social
  Security (income, starts later).
  3. UI:
    - Left: Accounts tree (collapsible, click-to-edit) replacing the old globals
   panel.
    - Center: Chart with draggable event nodes on the age axis (the #1 fix).
    - Right: Inspector that switches between Account editor (when an account is
  selected, showing inherited vs explicit values) and Event editor (when a node
  is selected, showing attached accounts + parameters).
  4. Engine: tree-aware resolution of inherited values; cashflow flows from
  income accounts into a designated cash account, drawn down by expense
  accounts; otherwise same yearly loop.

  Updating the mental-model memory with the tree detail, then I'll rebuild. Will
   report back when it's running on the same URL.

       30 +// Resolve a field via inheritance. Walk the chain, find the f
          +irst node                                                     
       31 +// where the field is set; apply override semantics if encount

       32 +function resolveField(                                        
       33 +  map: AccountMap,                                            
       34 +  id: string,                                                 
       35 +  field: keyof AccountNode,                                   
       36 +): number | undefined {                                       
       37 +  const chain = parentChain(map, id);                         
       38 +  // Walk root → leaf so we can apply child overrides on top o
          +f parent values.                                              
       39 +  let value: number | undefined;                              
       40 +  for (let i = chain.length - 1; i >= 0; i--) {               
       41 +    const v = chain[i][field] as FieldValue;                  
       42 +    if (v === undefined) continue;                            
       43 +    if (typeof v === 'number') value = v;

⏺ v2 is up at the same URL — http://localhost:5173/. Hard-refresh to clear the
  broken HMR state from when EnvironmentPanel was still mounted.

  What changed (the big stuff):

  - Three-pane layout: Accounts tree | chart + summary | Inspector. The
  "globals" panel is gone — what used to be sliders is now a subtree of nodes
  ("US Economy" parent with children for equity_yield, bond_yield, cash_yield,
  real_estate, inflation; "Tax" parent with California and Florida siblings).
  - Drag events on the timeline directly. One-shots are pill-shaped pins; ranged
   events (Roth ladder) are bars with two end-handles + a drag-the-whole-thing
  body. Cursor turns to grab/grabbing. Click without drag selects the event in
  the inspector.
  - Inspector opens whatever you click. Click an account to edit its values
  (Override checkbox toggles between inherited and explicit). Click an event to
  edit name, age range, parameters, and which accounts it's attached to.

  Seed scenario depth (so you can poke):

  - US Economy → equity 7%, bond 4%, cash 3.5%, real-estate 3.5%, inflation 3%
  - Tax → California (32% blended) vs Florida (22%)
  - Personal → Schwab {MSFT lot, VTI, BND}, Fidelity 401k {company-stock NUA
  candidate, index, bonds}, Vanguard Roth, Cash, House (Palo Alto), Salary,
  Social Security, Living expenses, Healthcare
  - 6 events seeded: Retire@67 (auto-generated from salary.end_age=67, marked
  with ✱), NUA on company stock@64, Roth ladder 65–72, Move to FL@70, Downsize
  house@75, Market downturn@72 attached to all four equity holdings

  Engine now does:
  - Tree-based inheritance: holding's growth comes from the matching ambient
  node by asset class
  - Income/expense streams flow through the cash sink with inflation indexing
  and COLA
  - Withdrawal sequencing when cash goes negative: taxable → tax-deferred →
  tax-free
  - Liquidate respects $500k MFJ exclusion on real estate, NUA-style
  ordinary-on-basis + LTCG-on-gains for tax-deferred
  - Reparent action swaps the actor's active jurisdiction (the FL move actually
  changes the tax rate used for subsequent calculations)
  - Market downturn applies the shared shock parameter (-25%) to each attached
  holding

  Things to push on:

  1. Does the accounts tree match how you actually think about your finances?
  Specifically the ambient-as-tree-node idea ("US Economy" is just another
  account) — does that read naturally or feel forced?
  2. Drag interaction. Single events should drag horizontally smoothly; ranged
  events should let you drag start/end/whole-thing. Is the affordance obvious?
  3. Inheritance UI. When you click an asset, the yield field shows "inherited
  7.0%" with an Override checkbox. Click override and the slider becomes active.
   Does that read right, or do you want explicit absolute-vs-delta toggle
  visible too?
  4. Auto-generated events are marked with ✱ next to the name. Does seeing the
  synthesized event satisfy "I declared end_age=67 and the system showed me what
   that means," or does it feel weird that there's a duplicate concept?
  5. Real-world feel. Can you imagine modeling your situation here — adding your
   real holdings, cost basis, expected events? What's missing that would make
  this fail the validation goal?

✻ Sautéed for 9m 50s

※ recap: Designing an event-driven personal finance modeler; just shipped v2 of the main-view interactive mock with a unified account-tree model, draggable timeline events, and a click-to-edit inspector at http://localhost:5173/. Next: you hard-refresh, poke at it, and tell me what feels off. (disable recaps in /config)

❯ 1- I think it's close. I would probably have the values of the US Economy     
  show up in the inspector rather than looking like accounts and I would parent 
   other accounts under that. The financial institution relationship is         
  probably best as a separate hierarchy or tagging mechanism because some       
  accounts in a given financial institution will be tied to one economy vs.     
  another.                                                                      
  2- The event dragging is better but not ideal. I think you're trying to pack  
  too much information into a limited space. I suggest showing events as        
  colored vertical lines on the main graph with an obvious "click me" node to   
  grab and possibly focus. Maybe move the inspector below and have it be a time 
   ordered list with a focused element expanded to edit.                        
  3- The override option works great.                                           
  4- No opinion yet.                                                            
  5- yes. This is definitely what I was thinking.                               

⏺ Got it. Two significant v3 changes:

  Tree restructure — fold the per-asset-class ambient nodes (amb_equity,
  amb_bond, etc.) into US Economy itself as scalar fields visible in its
  inspector. Personal accounts hang directly under US Economy. Custodian (Schwab
   / Fidelity / Vanguard) becomes a tag on each account, not a tree node —
  different accounts at the same custodian can inherit from different economies,
   so it doesn't belong in the inheritance hierarchy.

  Event UX redesign — drop the pin strip above the chart. Each event becomes a
  colored vertical line on the chart with a clickable/draggable node at the top.
   Below the chart, a time-ordered list of all events, with the selected one
  expanded inline showing its editor. Selection stays in sync across chart node
  ↔ list row ↔ vertical line. Right pane stays the account inspector.

  Saving the design lesson ("scalar attributes belong in the inspector, not as
  separate child nodes") and the custodian-as-tag decision. Building now.

  Wrote 3 memories (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ v3 is up. Refresh http://localhost:5173/.

  Changes:

  - Tree — flat: US Economy with all personal accounts as direct children, plus
  California / Florida as separate jurisdiction siblings. No more amb_equity /
  amb_bond / etc. — those are scalar fields on US Economy now, visible only when
   you click US Economy in the inspector. Custodian (Schwab, Fidelity 401(k),
  Vanguard Roth) shows as a tag in the inspector header on each account, not in
  the tree.
  - Chart — events are vertical lines spanning the chart's full height,
  color-coded (blue=one-shot, green=recurring, gray=auto-generated,
  gold=hovered/selected). For ranged events, both start and end show as vertical
   lines connected by a thin band above the plot area.
  - Draggable nodes above the chart — circles for one-shot events, square
  handles for ranged event start/end. Drag horizontally; the node hops by whole
  years.
  - Event timeline list below the chart — sorted by trigger age. Each row shows
  dot · age · name · summary · caret. Click to expand inline; click again to
  collapse. Selection syncs three-way: chart node ↔ vertical line ↔ list row all
   highlight together.
  - Right inspector is now accounts-only. When nothing is selected, it shows a
  placeholder pointing you to the timeline below.
  - Engine walks parents to find the typed yield field — so an equity holding
  with yield_rate = undefined inherits whatever value equity_yield has on the
  nearest ancestor (US Economy by default). Override checkbox on a holding's
  Yield field swaps to an explicit value.

  What to push on now:

  1. Tree feel — does the flat US Economy + jurisdictions structure read better
  than v2's nested ambient nodes?
  2. Inspector for US Economy — click the US Economy row. Are the four yields +
  inflation a reasonable set of scalars for that node, or are they mis-grouped
  (e.g., should inflation be a sibling node since it's conceptually different
  from yields)?
  3. Vertical event lines + draggable nodes — does the line-with-dot feel like
  the right primitive? Compared to v2's pin strip, is this clearer or more
  cluttered when many events are near each other (especially ages 64-72 where 4
  events cluster)?
  4. Event list expand-on-click — does inline expansion read well, or would you
  prefer the editor in a fixed pane that updates as you click rows? Inline keeps
   things in one place but can feel jumpy if the expanded section is tall.
  5. Custodian as inspector tag only — sufficient, or do you want a "View by
  custodian" toggle on the tree?

✻ Worked for 7m 32s

※ recap: We're prototyping an event-driven personal finance modeler and just shipped v3 of the main-view mock with a flatter account tree, chart-anchored event lines, and an expandable event timeline. Refresh localhost:5173 and tell me what works or doesn't.

❯ 1- This works well.                                                           
  2- I think these all work well together. More generally, it's useful to be    
  able to create a parent node that groups many value trends. It's easy enough  
  for the user to zero them out.                                                
  3- This works well. The events that span time are a bit noisy. A few thoughts 
   on this below.                                                               
  4- This works ok but I think it will be better to use the right side panel    
  inspector for editing events. Less jumping around and a consistent pattern.   
  5- Maybe a view by custodian toggle in the future but no immediate need. I    
  think tags will be a simple way for the user to annotate things without       
  creating complexity in the UI.                                                
                                                                                
  More thoughts on the timeline view.                                           
  - The main panel will need zoom.                                              
  - I think the top row will get busy and confusing. I suggest that we show     
  vertical event lines (or dots) on the main timeline which highlight the event 
   list below. The event list could have two modes: list and timeline which     
  would be a gannt-like series of time spans. Alternatively these could stack   
  in the main timeline view but that would convey some association with the net 
   worth total. Confusing. For now it's fine unless you have suggestions.       
                                                                                
  Other thoughts. I'd like to be able to model expenses and income with rates   
  of increase or decrease. Much like an asset. A job is an asset that produces  
  annual income which can increase year-over-year. Similarly, expenses can      
  increase over time.                                                           

⏺ Got it. v4 plan:

  - Inspector handles events again (consistency win — the inline expansion was
  clever but inconsistent with the account pattern).
  - Cleaner chart event display — drop the connecting bar above the plot; keep
  just clean vertical lines + draggable circle/handle nodes. Ranged events get
  two thin lines.
  - Event timeline list gets a List ↔ Timeline toggle. Timeline mode is a
  gantt-style horizontal layout with bars whose width = duration. Both modes are
   pure selectors; editing happens in the right Inspector.
  - Horizontal chart zoom via recharts <Brush> so you can focus on a sub-range
  of ages without losing the full picture.
  - Income/expense growth rates as a first-class field. Job income compounds at
  e.g. 3% raises/yr; expenses at e.g. 3% inflation; SS at 2.5% COLA. Generalizes
   the existing cola_rate and applies to all streams.

  Saving a couple of design notes (events-and-accounts share the right
  Inspector; deferred custodian-view-toggle), then building.

  Wrote 2 memories (ctrl+o to expand)

      124 +  const span = maxAge - minAge;                               
      125 +  const tickAges: number[] = [];                              
      126 +  for (let a = Math.ceil(minAge / 5) * 5; a <= maxAge; a += 5)
          + tickAges.push(a);                                            
      127 +                                                              
      128 +  return (                                                    
      129 +    <div className="gantt">                                   
      130 +      <div className="gantt-axis">                            
      131 +        {tickAges.map((age) => {                              
      132 +          const pct = ((age - minAge) / span) * 100;          
      133 +          return (                                            
      134 +            <div key={age} className="gantt-tick" style={{ lef
          +t: `${pct}%` }}>                                              
      135 +              <div className="gantt-tick-mark" />             
      136 +              <div className="gantt-tick-label">{age}</div>   
      137 +            </div>                                            
      138 +          );                                                  
      139 +        })}                                                   
      140 +      </div>                                                  
      141 +      <div className="gantt-rows">                            
      142 +        {events.map((e) => {                                  
      143 +          const startPct = ((e.trigger_age - minAge) / span) *
          + 100;                                                         
      144 +          const isRange = e.end_age !== undefined && e.end_age
          + !== e.trigger_age;                                           
      145 +          const endPct = isRange                              
      146 +            ? ((e.end_age! - minAge) / span) * 100            
      147 +            : startPct;                                       
      148 +          const widthPct = Math.max(endPct - startPct, 0.6);  
      149 +          const sel = isSelected(e.id);                       
      150 +          const hov = hoveredId === e.id;                     
      151 +          return (                                            
      152 +            <div                                              
      153 +              key={e.id}                                      
      154 +              className={`gantt-row ${sel ? 'on' : ''} ${hov ?
          + 'hovered' : ''}`}                                            
      155 +              onMouseEnter={() => onHover(e.id)}              
      156 +              onMouseLeave={() => onHover(null)}              
      157 +            >                                                 
      158 +              <div className="gantt-row-track">               
      159 +                <div                                          
      160 +                  className={`gantt-bar ${e.kind} ${isRange ? 
          +'range' : 'point'}`}                                          
      161 +                  style={{ left: `${startPct}%`, width: `${wid
          +thPct}%` }}                                                   
      162 +                  onClick={() => onSelect(e.id)}              
      163 +                  title={`${e.name} (${e.trigger_age}${isRange
          + ? `–${e.end_age}` : ''})`}                                   
      164 +                >                                             
      165 +                  <span className="gantt-bar-label">          
      166 +                    {e.name}                                  
      167 +                    {e.auto_generated ? ' ✱' : ''}            
      168 +                  </span>                                     
      169 +                </div>                                        
      170 +              </div>                                          
      171 +            </div>                                            
      172 +          );                                                  
      173 +        })}                                                   
      174 +      </div>                                                  
      175 +    </div>                                                    
      176 +  );                                                          
      177 +}

⏺ v4 is up. Refresh http://localhost:5173/.

  Changes shipped:

  1. Income/expense growth rates — every income and expense account has an
  Annual growth rate field. Salary at 3% (annual raises), SS at 2.5% (COLA),
  Living expenses at 3%, Healthcare at 5% (medical inflation). Engine compounds
  the base annual amount over time. Click Salary or Healthcare in the tree to
  see and tune it.
  2. Inspector edits events again. Click any event row in the list (or the dot
  above the chart, or its vertical line if hoverable) → it opens in the right
  Inspector. Account editing pattern and event editing pattern are now
  identical.
  3. List ↔ Timeline mode toggle at the top right of the event list:
    - List: vertical rows sorted by trigger age. Click to select.
    - Timeline (gantt): each event is a horizontal bar on a shared age axis. Bar
   width = duration. Recurring events show as green bars; one-shot events show
  as small ticks. Click → select. (Bars are not yet draggable in gantt mode —
  they're a visualization layer; drag still happens via the dots above the
  chart.)
  4. Cleaner chart for ranged events — dropped the colored band that was
  floating above the plot. Range events now show as just two dots above the
  chart and one vertical line at the start; the second (end) line only renders
  when the event is hovered or selected. Big drop in idle visual noise.
  5. Chart zoom (Brush) — deferred to v5. I started it but the math for keeping
  the draggable nodes aligned with brush-zoomed x-axis is non-trivial enough
  that I'd rather get your read on the rest of v4 first. The Gantt mode
  partially scratches the same itch (dedicated horizontal time view).

  What to push on:

  1. Gantt mode — does it solve "see all events in time" without cluttering the
  main chart? Or do you want the bars draggable too (i.e., gantt becomes a
  primary editing surface, not just a viz)?
  2. Growth rate on income/expense — slide Salary's growth rate up to 6% or down
   to 0% and watch the chart redraw. Does the model behave as expected?
  3. Event Inspector — same right pane now handles both accounts and events.
  Less jumpy?
  4. Ranged event chart display — quieter? Click the Roth ladder dot to surface
  its end line. Reasonable behavior, or do you want both lines visible always
  (just thinner)?
  5. Anything else missing for the validation goal (could you model your

⏺ Thanks — the "let's discuss" question is the most important one of this round,
   so let me lay out a concrete proposal before building.

  Tax & cash-flow visibility — the actual problem:

  Right now the engine does compute taxes, forced asset sales (when cash goes
  negative), and event-driven liquidations (NUA, house sale, Roth conversion
  taxes), but none of those are visible. So you can't actually evaluate "Roth
  conversion at 65 costs me $25k more tax this year but saves $40k later" — the
  whole story is hidden behind the single net-worth line.

  Proposal: Cash-flow bar chart below the net-worth chart, sharing the age 
  x-axis.

  Per year, two-direction stacked bars:
  - Above zero (inflows): each income source as its own segment (salary, SS),
  event liquidation proceeds (NUA at 64, house sale at 75), forced sale proceeds
   (when expenses force a withdrawal — distinct color so it reads as "this was
  forced, not earned").
  - Below zero (outflows): each expense source (living expenses, healthcare),
  and taxes paid that year.

  That makes the failure modes you can't currently see:
  - Tax spikes during Roth conversion years 65–72; tax drop after FL move at 70
  - The salary-ends-but-SS-hasn't-started gap at ages 67–69 → forced-sale bar
  appears
  - House sale at 75 = big positive bar + corresponding tax spike

  Hover a year for a numeric breakdown.

  Bundle of polish items per your other notes:
  - Drop Gantt mode (scrapping as you said).
  - Translucent ReferenceArea on the chart spanning the age range when a ranged
  event is selected — that's the alpha overlay you described.
  - Selection ↔ chart sync so clicking an event in the list also highlights its
  line(s) on the chart (currently only hover does).
  - Tooltips on the chart vertical lines showing event name + age + summary.

  One open question:
  - Tax breakdown granularity. I'll start with one "Taxes" segment per year
  (everything aggregated). Worth splitting into ordinary income / capital gains
  / Roth-conversion tax later, but more code. OK to start coarse?

  Going to build this now unless you want to redirect. Will report back once
  it's running.

⏺ v5 is up. Refresh http://localhost:5173/.

  What's new:

  1. Cash flow bar chart below the net worth chart, sharing the age x-axis.
  Stacked bars per year:
    - Green/teal segments above zero — Salary (62–66), Social Security (70+)
    - Cyan spike — Event liquidations (NUA at 64, house sale at 75)
    - Orange — Forced sales (asset withdrawals when expenses outrun
  income/cash). Watch ages 67–69 — the gap between salary ending and SS starting
   forces sales.
    - Red/orange below zero — Living expenses, Healthcare (starts at 65)
    - Dark red — Taxes. Watch the spike during Roth conversion years 65–72, the
  drop at 70 when FL move kicks in, the big spike at 75 from house sale.
    - Hover any bar for full breakdown
  2. Selected ranged events get a translucent gold overlay on the net worth
  chart spanning the age range. Click "Roth conversion ladder" in the event list
   — you'll see ages 65–72 highlighted across both charts.
  3. Selection ↔ chart sync — clicking an event in the list now highlights its
  lines on both charts (previously only hover did this).
  4. Tooltips on event nodes above the chart now include the description.
  5. Gantt mode dropped as you said.

  Things to push on:

  1. Cash flow chart legibility — does the stacked-bar story tell what you
  wanted? Especially: is the orange "forced sale" segment clearly
  distinguishable from the cyan "event liquidation" one? Both are positive-axis
  cash inflows but mean very different things.
  2. The Roth conversion story — slide the Roth ladder's amount parameter
  up/down (in the Inspector) and watch the tax bars during 65–72 grow/shrink,
  and the long-term net worth respond. Does this validate the "explore
  tax-planning trade-offs" goal?
  3. Tax breakdown granularity — single "Taxes" segment OK, or do you want it
  split (ordinary income vs LTCG vs conversion vs event)?
  4. Forced sales in the seed — with current income/expenses, the gap years
  67–69 should produce visible orange bars. Is that the right mental model
  (forced asset liquidation), or would you rather see this as "explicit
  withdrawal events" the user has to place themselves?
  5. Chart stack — anything missing? Do you want a separate net-of-everything
  line? RMDs (not modeled yet)? IRMAA? Insurance premiums?

✻ Baked for 6m 0s

※ recap: Building an interactive React mock of an event-driven personal-finance modeler to validate the unified ledger model and UI. Just shipped v5 with a cash-flow bar chart showing taxes and forced sales; next, get your read on whether it tells the tax-planning story you wanted.

❯ 1- this is great.                                                             
  2- yes, perfect                                                               
  3- I think a breakdown of income vs. LTCG would be easy enough but I don't    
  know that it's easily discerned when looking at the bar charts over a long    
  period of time. What do you think about showing this breakdown in the         
  tooltip. We could simplify the income vs. expenses bar chart and then show a  
  detailed chart in the tooltip.                                                
  4- Forced sales works. If the user wants to time the asset sales then they    
  can add a manual asset sale earlier on the timeline and create additional     
  cash such that the forced asset sale isn't needed. Right?                     
  5- We should definitely model RMDs but that's fine for the mock. There are    
  nuances about tax-deductable expenses (e.g. health insurance premiums) vs.    
  normal expenses (cars insurance) but that feels like too much complexity for  
  this tool. What do you think?                                                 

⏺ Three answers, then v6:

  On tax breakdown (3): Agree — keep the bar chart simple, put the detail in the
   tooltip. Easier to read at-a-glance, full story when you hover. I'll split
  into Ordinary vs LTCG since that's what you asked, and present the whole
  year's breakdown (income sources, outflow sources, tax with ordinary/LTCG
  split, net cash flow) in a custom tooltip.

  On forced sales (4): Yes, exactly that mechanism. A user-placed liquidate
  event in, say, age 66 would proactively sell taxable assets to top up cash;
  the engine then has enough cash for ages 67–69 and the orange "forced sale"
  bars disappear. Forced sale is the safety net that visually says "you didn't
  plan for this gap." This is the right model.

  On tax-deductible expenses (5): Agree — defer. The reason: we're using a
  single effective tax rate, which already implicitly bundles "all my deductions
   averaged in." Adding a tax_deductible flag now would double-count the tax
  savings unless we also move to bracket-based math. So bundle it with the
  eventual upgrade to brackets/IRMAA. For now, users who want to pressure-test
  deductibility just lower the effective rate.

  Saving the chart-summary / tooltip-detail design pattern, then building v6.

  Wrote 2 memories (ctrl+o to expand)

      186 +interface CashFlowTooltipProps {                              
      187 +  active?: boolean;                                           
      188 +  label?: number;                                             
      189 +  projection: YearlyProjection[];                             
      190 +  nameById: Record<string, string>;                           
      191 +  dollarMode: 'nominal' | 'real';                             
      192  }
      193  
      194 +function CashFlowTooltip({                                    
      195 +  active,                                                     
      196 +  label,                                                      
      197 +  projection,                                                 
      198 +  nameById,                                                   
      199 +  dollarMode,                                                 
      200 +}: CashFlowTooltipProps): JSX.Element | null {                
      201 +  if (!active || label === undefined) return null;            
      202 +  const p = projection.find((x) => x.age === label);          
      203 +  if (!p) return null;                                        
      204 +  const div = dollarMode === 'real' ? p.cumulative_inflation_i
          +ndex : 1;                                                     
      205 +                                                              
      206 +  const incomeEntries = Object.entries(p.income_by_source).fil
          +ter(                                                          
      207 +    ([, v]) => v > 0,                                         
      208 +  );                                                          
      209 +  const expenseEntries = Object.entries(p.expense_by_source).f
          +ilter(                                                        
      210 +    ([, v]) => v > 0,                                         
      211 +  );                                                          
      212 +  const totalIncomeSources = incomeEntries.reduce((s, [, v]) =
          +> s + v, 0) / div;                                            
      213 +  const eventLiq = p.event_liquidation_proceeds / div;        
      214 +  const forcedSale = p.forced_sale_proceeds / div;            
      215 +  const totalIn = totalIncomeSources + eventLiq + forcedSale; 
      216 +  const totalOut =                                            
      217 +    Object.values(p.expense_by_source).reduce((s, v) => s + v,
          + 0) / div +                                                   
      218 +    p.taxes_paid / div;                                       
      219 +  const net = totalIn - totalOut;                             
      220 +                                                              
      221 +  return (                                                    
      222 +    <div className="cf-tooltip">                              
      223 +      <div className="cf-tt-head">Age {p.age}</div>           
      224 +                                                              
      225 +      <div className="cf-tt-section">                         
      226 +        <div className="cf-tt-section-h">Income</div>         
      227 +        {incomeEntries.length === 0 && eventLiq === 0 && force
          +dSale === 0 && (                                              
      228 +          <div className="cf-tt-row muted">— none —</div>     
      229 +        )}                                                    
      230 +        {incomeEntries.map(([id, v]) => (                     
      231 +          <div key={id} className="cf-tt-row">                
      232 +            <span>{nameById[id] ?? id}</span>                 
      233 +            <span>{fmtMoney(v / div)}</span>                  
      234 +          </div>                                              
      235 +        ))}                                                   
      236 +        {eventLiq > 0 && (                                    
      237 +          <div className="cf-tt-row">                         
      238 +            <span>Event liquidation (net)</span>              
      239 +            <span>{fmtMoney(eventLiq)}</span>                 
      240 +          </div>                                              
      241 +        )}                                                    
      242 +        {forcedSale > 0 && (                                  
      243 +          <div className="cf-tt-row">                         
      244 +            <span className="cf-warn">Forced asset sale</span>
      245 +            <span>{fmtMoney(forcedSale)}</span>               
      246 +          </div>                                              
      247 +        )}                                                    
      248 +        <div className="cf-tt-total">                         
      249 +          <span>Total in</span>                               
      250 +          <span>{fmtMoney(totalIn)}</span>                    
      251 +        </div>                                                
      252 +      </div>                                                  
      253 +                                                              
      254 +      <div className="cf-tt-section">                         
      255 +        <div className="cf-tt-section-h">Outflows</div>       
      256 +        {expenseEntries.length === 0 && p.taxes_paid === 0 && 
          +(                                                             
      257 +          <div className="cf-tt-row muted">— none —</div>     
      258 +        )}                                                    
      259 +        {expenseEntries.map(([id, v]) => (                    
      260 +          <div key={id} className="cf-tt-row">                
      261 +            <span>{nameById[id] ?? id}</span>                 
      262 +            <span>{fmtMoney(v / div)}</span>                  
      263 +          </div>                                              
      264 +        ))}                                                   
      265 +        {p.taxes_paid > 0 && (                                
      266 +          <div className="cf-tt-tax">                         
      267 +            <div className="cf-tt-row">                       
      268 +              <span>Taxes</span>                              
      269 +              <span>{fmtMoney(p.taxes_paid / div)}</span>     
      270 +            </div>                                            
      271 +            <div className="cf-tt-subrow">                    
      272 +              <span>· ordinary income</span>                  
      273 +              <span>{fmtMoney(p.tax_ordinary / div)}</span>   
      274 +            </div>                                            
      275 +            <div className="cf-tt-subrow">                    
      276 +              <span>· LTCG / qualified</span>                 
      277 +              <span>{fmtMoney(p.tax_ltcg / div)}</span>       
      278 +            </div>                                            
      279 +          </div>                                              
      280 +        )}                                                    
      281 +        <div className="cf-tt-total">                         
      282 +          <span>Total out</span>                              
      283 +          <span>{fmtMoney(totalOut)}</span>                   
      284 +        </div>                                                
      285 +      </div>                                                  
      286 +                                                              
      287 +      <div className={`cf-tt-net ${net >= 0 ? 'pos' : 'neg'}`}
          +>                                                             
      288 +        <span>Net cash flow</span>                            
      289 +        <span>{(net >= 0 ? '+' : '') + fmtMoney(net)}</span>  
      290 +      </div>                                                  
      291 +    </div>                                                    
      292 +  );                                                          
      293 +}                                                             
      294 +                                                              
      295  interface LegendProps {
      296    incomeIds: string[];
      297    expenseIds: string[];

⏺ v6 is up. Refresh http://localhost:5173/.

  What's new:

  - Custom rich tooltip on the cash flow chart. Hover any year's bar — you get:
    - Income section: each income source listed (Salary / Social Security), plus
   "Event liquidation (net)" and "Forced asset sale" when applicable, with
  subtotal.
    - Outflows section: each expense source, plus a "Taxes" line that breaks out
   into ordinary income vs LTCG/qualified subrows.
    - Net cash flow at the bottom — green if positive, red if negative. The
  years where you see the most red here are exactly the years where forced sales
   appear in the bar above.
  - Engine now tracks ordinary vs LTCG separately through every code path:
  salary → ordinary; SS → ordinary; Roth conversion → ordinary; taxable account
  liquidation → LTCG; tax-deferred liquidation (NUA/forced) → split (basis at
  ordinary, gains at LTCG); house sale → LTCG with $500k MFJ exclusion.
  - The bar chart itself is unchanged — single "Taxes" segment as we agreed,
  summary in chart, detail in tooltip.

  Worked examples to confirm the math:

  - Hover age 64 (NUA year): Taxes should split into a chunk of ordinary (basis
  $80k × ~32% ≈ $26k) plus a smaller chunk of LTCG (gain $320k × ~19% ≈ $60k).
  Big "Event liquidation" entry on the income side.
  - Hover ages 65–69: Salary running out, Roth conversion taxes adding to
  ordinary every year. Healthcare kicks in at 65.
  - Hover ages 67–69: Salary ended, SS hasn't started — should see "Forced asset
   sale" in income (orange) and red Net cash flow.
  - Hover age 70: SS starts, FL move drops the tax rate, conversion still
  running.
  - Hover age 75: House sale — big LTCG (most absorbed by $500k MFJ exclusion) +
   big "Event liquidation (net)" entry.

  Things to push on:

  1. Tooltip layout — does the income/outflows/net structure read cleanly, or is
   it dense? Subrow indent for ordinary/LTCG fits or feels awkward?
  2. Net cash flow line — does that single number help (red = "this year ate
  into assets") or is it redundant with the bar chart?
  3. Validation goal check — with this in place, can you now actually see the
  trade-offs you'd want to model? E.g., slide Roth ladder to $0 and watch which
  tax bars shrink, then see the long-term effect on net worth. Does it feel like
   a real tool or still mock-y?
  4. Anything missing for the validation goal before we declare the main view
  good and move to the next mock (probably the event composer / new-event
  creation flow, or scenario compare)?

⏺ v6.1 fixes are live. Refresh http://localhost:5173/.

  Two fixes:

  1. Net cash flow line should now be visible. The custom tooltip is taller than
   the chart's plot area, and recharts was clipping the bottom. Added
  allowEscapeViewBox={{ y: true }} so it can render past the chart bounds, plus
  zIndex: 100 so it floats above other content.
  2. Market downturn drag bug fixed. I had a clamp asymmetry — the 'whole' drag
  handle clamped to minAge on the lower bound but not to maxAge on the upper. So
   if you dragged the dot rightward past age 95, the event silently sat at age
  96+ where the simulation never reaches it, producing zero visible effect. Now
  clamped on both ends, and ranged events also stay fully in-bounds.

  To verify the market dip is working (now that it can't slip off-chart): grab
  the Market downturn dot at age 72 and drag it left and right. You should see a
   clear notch in the net worth line move with the dot. With the default −25%
  shock, net worth drops about 11–16% at the shock year (verified directly
  against the engine: at age 72, $7.68M → $6.79M).

  If after this fix you still don't see a clear dip, that points to a different
  bug and I'll dig deeper. But I expect this was the cause.

  We're at a good stopping point on the main view: model validated, all the
  major UX patterns settled, no concrete blockers you've flagged. Likely next
  mocks: scenario compare (overlay two scenarios — Roth conversion vs no Roth
  conversion is the killer demo) or event composer / new-account creation flow
  (right now everything's pre-seeded; we haven't validated the
  create-from-scratch experience). Your call which one to tackle next.

⏺ I can see the bug clearly in your screenshot — look at the inspector: Trigger 
  age 80.7650240384.... That's not an integer. The drag is producing a
  fractional age, and eventFires checks trigger_age === age against integer
  ages, so the event never fires. Found it.

  The root cause is in startDrag: I capture grabAtAge: xToAge(x) without
  rounding. Then delta = age - grabAtAge is fractional, and initialStart + delta
   becomes fractional. Need to round at the boundary.

  For the tooltip — bigger picture issue is the cashflow chart sits inside a
  overflow-y: auto container, so the tooltip extends beyond and gets clipped.
  I'll move the Net line to the top of the tooltip so the most important number
  is always visible regardless of clipping, and compact the rest.

⏺ v6.2 fixes are live. Two bugs squashed:

  1. The market downturn bug — root cause confirmed. Your screenshot showed
  Trigger age: 80.7650240384... That fractional age can never match an integer
  year in the simulation, so the event silently never fired. The cause was
  startDrag capturing grabAtAge: xToAge(x) without rounding — every drag
  accumulated fractional drift. Fixed in two places (defense in depth):
  - startDrag rounds grabAtAge
  - setEventAge rounds at the store boundary so any caller is safe

  To recover your current state: either refresh the page (resets the seed,
  market_dip back to age 72) or just grab the orange dot in the chart and drag
  it — the next drag will round.

  2. Net cash flow line — restructured. The custom tooltip was getting clipped
  by the parent's overflow-y: auto. Moved the Net cash flow to the header of the
   tooltip, right next to the age — color-coded green/red. So even if the rest
  of the tooltip is clipped at the bottom, the most important number is always
  visible at the top. Also compacted the body (smaller fonts, dropped redundant
  subtotal lines, totals now inline in section headers).

  ---
  Agreed: next mock = event composer / new-account creation. Here's my proposed
  scope:

  v7 — Mock #3 build plan:

  1. Add account flow:
    - "+ Add account" button at top of the tree (and possibly per-parent for
  nesting under specific economy/jurisdiction)
    - Inline kind picker: asset / income / expense / ambient
    - Selecting kind creates a stub account with sensible defaults, opens it in
  the Inspector for further editing
    - Delete button in the Inspector for the selected account
  2. Add event flow:
    - "+ Add event" button at top of the event timeline list
    - Template gallery — small set of pre-built event blueprints: Roth
  conversion ladder, Move to new state, Sell asset, Market shock, NUA execution,
   Salary change. User picks a template, fills in the specifics (which accounts,
   what amount, what age range), event gets added to the timeline.
    - "Custom event" option for power users — pick action type(s), attach
  accounts, parameterize from scratch.
    - Delete button in the Inspector for the selected event.
  3. Auto-events revisit — when an income/expense account has end_age set,
  ensure the auto-generated end-event shows up in the timeline. Today this is
  hardcoded in seed; v7 should make it real.

  The trickiest UX question is the template gallery: how curated do templates
  feel vs how flexible. I'd bias toward a small, curated set with a "Custom"
  escape hatch — easier to validate than a generic builder.

  Does this scope match what you had in mind, or is there a slice of this you
  want to focus on (e.g., just account creation first, leave events for later)?

⏺ Written to docs/DESIGN_NOTES.md. Twelve sections; treats existing PRD/DESIGN
  as historical and supersedes them where they conflict. Self-contained — a
  fresh implementer (or me with no transcript) can read it and proceed.

  What's in it:

  1. Product in one paragraph — who it's for, what they're trying to do
  2. Mental model — 7 non-negotiables (unified ledger, tree inheritance, events
  transform attached accounts, sliders edit starting values, holdings
  first-class, custodian as tag, scalars in inspector)
  3. Data schema — full TS types for AccountNode, TimelineEvent, ActionTemplate,
   Actor, YearlyProjection, plus the schema decisions worth flagging
  4. Engine contract — pure function signature, annual loop steps, cone math
  (horizon-shock not annual ind. vol), tax model + the known limitation,
  ordinary/LTCG bucketing table, withdrawal sequencing
  5. UX architecture — layout diagram, key components, 9 hard rules from lessons
   learned, plus the explicit list of patterns to never retry (sliders-in-list,
  pin strip, inline expand, gantt mode, connecting bar)
  6. Real-vs-Nominal toggle — the gotcha about not double-counting inflation
  7. Out of scope for V1 — table with notes for when each becomes worth adding
  8. Implementation gotchas — drag rounding, drag clamping, useEffect deps,
  tooltip clipping, three-scenario simulation, empty-attachment handling, the
  Math.abs(v) < 1 heuristic
  9. Open questions — override syntax, auto-event materialization vs derivation,
   template gallery vs custom events
  10. Tech stack used
  11. What mock #1 validated
  12. What mock #1 did not validate (i.e., next mock targets)

  The lessons section is the highest-value part for "starting fresh" — it
  captures the four UI patterns we tried and threw away with rationale, so the
  next implementation doesn't waste cycles re-deriving them.

  Want to read it through and call out anything I got wrong or missing before we
   start mock #2 (account/event composer, no templates)?

❯ Would screenshots help, possibly annotated?                                   

⏺ Honest take: yes for a few specific things, no as a default.

  Screenshots are highest-leverage for two cases:

  1. Things hard to describe in text — the
  chart-with-vertical-lines-and-draggable-nodes-on-top, the translucent
  ReferenceArea on a selected ranged event, the rich tooltip with the colored
  Net header. Text + ASCII can sketch the layout but doesn't convey the visual 
  hierarchy that the design depends on.
  2. Anchoring "what good looks like" for a fresh implementer — one annotated
  hero shot is worth a paragraph.

  Screenshots are NOT worth it for:
  - Every section. Most of the doc is decisions, not pixels.
  - Anti-patterns. We deleted those components (Gantt mode, pin strip, inline
  expansion, sliders-in-list); resurrecting them just for screenshots is more
  work than it's worth. The text descriptions are sharp enough that future-me
  won't be tempted.

  A focused set of 4 candidates:

  1. Hero shot — full app with three panes labeled (AccountsTree /
  Charts+Timeline / Inspector), one event selected so the chart highlight, list
  highlight, and Inspector content all line up.
  2. Net-worth chart with a ranged event selected — shows draggable nodes above
  the plot, vertical start/end lines, the gold ReferenceArea spanning the range.
  3. Cash-flow chart with rich tooltip open — shows segment composition (income
  above, outflows below) and the tooltip's Net-at-the-top + breakdown structure.
  4. Inspector for an event mid-edit — shows the parameters slider,
  attached-accounts checklist, actions list. Anchors the "lists/timelines
  select; Inspector edits" rule.

  Practical proposal:

  You take the four screenshots from the running mock. Save them as PNG in
  docs/images/. I'll embed them in DESIGN_NOTES.md with captions and the
  annotation intent (e.g., "Arrow → 'draggable node, age axis'"). If you want
  them actually annotated with arrows/labels, easiest is Preview.app's markup
  tools — macOS native, no other software. Or you ship raw and I describe
  annotations in the caption.

  Two side notes:

  - Mark screenshots as "reference snapshot from mock #1, not spec." Otherwise a
   future implementer pixel-copies stale UI.
  - Optional fifth shot: a hover state showing chart↔list↔Inspector selection
  sync. It's the single most impressive interaction and otherwise easy to miss
  in text.

  Want me to embed them once you have the PNGs, or would you rather skip and
  move to mock #2?

⏺ Now auto-event materialization. The decision: derive on every projection (not
  materialize once on save). Reasons: source of truth stays on the account; no
  two-place-edit problem; auto-events stay perfectly in sync with the source
  field. The cost is a bit of compute and that the user can't customize an
  auto-event without first making it explicit. Adding a small UI affordance for
  that conversion.

  Read 2 files (ctrl+o to expand)

  ⎿  Added 37 lines, removed 1 line
      302      }),
      303  }));
      304  
      305 +// Synthesize "auto-events" derived from declarative fields on
          + accounts.                                                    
      306 +// Today: end_account events for income/expense streams whose 
          +end_age is                                                    
      307 +// less than the actor's horizon_age. Derived on every read so
          + the source                                                   
      308 +// account remains the single source of truth.                
      309 +export function synthesizeAutoEvents(                         
      310 +  accounts: AccountNode[],                                    
      311 +  actor: Actor,                                               
      312 +): TimelineEvent[] {                                          
      313 +  const out: TimelineEvent[] = [];                            
      314 +  for (const a of accounts) {                                 
      315 +    if (a.kind !== 'income' && a.kind !== 'expense') continue;
      316 +    if (a.end_age === undefined) continue;                    
      317 +    if (a.end_age >= actor.horizon_age) continue;             
      318 +    out.push({                                                
      319 +      id: `auto_end_${a.id}`,                                 
      320 +      name: `End ${a.name.toLowerCase()}`,                    
      321 +      description: `Auto-generated from ${a.name}.end_age = ${
          +a.end_age}. Edit the source account to change.`,              
      322 +      trigger_age: a.end_age,                                 
      323 +      kind: 'one_shot',                                       
      324 +      attached_account_ids: [a.id],                           
      325 +      parameters: {},                                         
      326 +      actions: [{ type: 'end_account' }],                     
      327 +      auto_generated: true,                                   
      328 +    });                                                       
      329 +  }                                                           
      330 +  return out;                                                 
      331 +}                                                             
      332 +                                                              
      333 +export function useAllEvents(): TimelineEvent[] {             
      334 +  const accounts = useStore((s) => s.accounts);               
      335 +  const actor = useStore((s) => s.actor);                     
      336 +  const events = useStore((s) => s.events);                   
      337 +  return [...events, ...synthesizeAutoEvents(accounts, actor)]
          +;                                                             
      338 +}                                                             
      339 +                                                              
      340  export function useProjection(): YearlyProjection[] {
      341    const accounts = useStore((s) => s.accounts);
      342    const actor = useStore((s) => s.actor);
      343    const events = useStore((s) => s.events);
      309 -  return project(accounts, actor, events);                    
      344 +  const all = [...events, ...synthesizeAutoEvents(accounts, ac
          +tor)];                                                        
      345 +  return project(accounts, actor, all);                       
      346  }

⏺ v7 is up — refresh http://localhost:5173/. Mock #2 is in.

  What's new:

  1. Add account flow. "+ Add" button next to the Accounts header. Click → kind
  picker (Asset / Income / Expense / Ambient). Pick a kind → stub created with
  sensible defaults (asset = equity/taxable/$0, income = $0/yr from age 60–95,
  expense = $0/yr at 3% growth, ambient = root with default yields), parented
  under US Economy by default (or root for ambient), and auto-selected so you
  land in the Inspector for editing.
  2. Add event flow (no templates, custom only). "+ One-shot" / "+ Recurring"
  buttons in the timeline header. Click one → action picker grid: Liquidate,
  Transfer, Shock/adjust, Set value, Reparent, End stream. Pick an action → stub
   event created with a primary action wired up and sensible default parameters
  (e.g., transfer pre-wires amount=$50k, shock pre-wires shock=-10%).
  3. Action editor inside the EventInspector. Replaces the read-only <code>
  chips. Each action is a card with: type dropdown, conditional fields
  (target_account / new_parent / field / param_ref, depending on type), and a ×
  button to remove. "+ Add action" at the bottom lets you compose multi-action
  events. This is what makes the unified-ledger model actually expressive
  without templates — e.g., you can build an NUA from scratch by stacking
  liquidate + tax-aware transfer.
  4. Delete buttons in both the AccountInspector and EventInspector header.
  Account delete checks: blocked if the node has children, blocked if it's the
  actor's cash sink or jurisdiction. On success, removes the account and any
  event attachments to it. Event delete is unconstrained.
  5. Auto-event materialization (the "derive" answer). Resolved the open
  question by deriving on every projection rather than materializing once. The
  synthesizeAutoEvents() function in the store generates an end_account
  auto-event for every income/expense account with end_age < horizon_age. They
  appear in the timeline list, on both charts, and in the inspector — but with a
   ✱ badge and a read-only inspector explaining "edit the source account's
  end_age to change this." Drag is also disabled for them. The evt_retire seed
  entry (which manually duplicated the auto-event) is removed.

  To verify:
  - Add a new income account, set annual_amount=120000, end_age=70 → an
  auto-event "End new income stream" appears at age 70 in the list, marked ✱.
  - Click the auto-event → readonly inspector with "Open source account" button.
  - Click that button → jumps you to the income account so you can edit end_age.
  - Try to delete US Economy → blocked because it has children.
  - Try to delete cash_reserves → blocked because it's the actor's cash sink.

  Open design question for next round:

  The action editor is functional but a bit clinical — it exposes set_value,
  add_value, etc. as primitives. For someone unfamiliar with the schema, this is
   jarring. Two approaches when we eventually want it friendlier:

  - Verb-first phrasing in the action card head ("Sell asset" instead of
  liquidate, "Move money" instead of transfer, "Change tax jurisdiction" instead
   of reparent).
  - Templates (the deferred path) hide the primitive choice entirely behind
  named blueprints.

  Worth deciding before we ship for real, but not for this mock unless you want
  to push further.

  Want me to take a screenshot pass for the new flows and add them to
  DESIGN_NOTES.md, or move on to whatever you want next?
