❯ I think this is great. Good enough for a MVP, really. I agree that the action 
   editor is a bit clinical, I think it's enough for me right now. Improvements 
   can happen incrementally.                                                    
  Although I had talked about a fresh start, this feels good enough. What would 
   it take to turn this into a working app? How is the current code             
  architecture? Will it lend itself to ongoing development?                     

⏺ Honest assessment, in three parts.

  How is the current code architecture?

  Good — better than I expected when I started auditing. The shape is:

  seed.ts ──┐
            ├──→ store (state + mutators) ──→ useProjection (pure engine) ──→
  components
            ↓                                              ↑
         types.ts ←──────────────────────────────────────────

  What's structurally right:

  - Engine is pure. project(accounts, actor, events) → YearlyProjection[] has no
   side effects, no I/O, no React. You can snapshot-test it with seed data in 10
   lines. This is the most expensive thing to get right and we got it right.
  - Strict TypeScript everywhere. Including discriminated unions for Selection
  and FieldValue. Refactors are safe.
  - Components are dumb. Read from store, render. No business logic inside JSX.
  UI bugs are local.
  - Store is single source of truth. Mutators are small and obvious. Auto-events
   are derived, not stored — so we don't have a sync problem.
  - Selection model is one variable consumed by everyone. Chart highlight, list
  state, inspector content all read the same selection. Easy to extend.

  What's mock-quality but local (would not require restructuring to fix):

  - Engine re-runs on every render. Every component that calls useProjection()
  runs project() independently. Three components do. That's ~3× cone simulations
   × 33 years × 20 accounts per render, and slider drags fire many renders/sec.
  Fix is one useMemo (or a zustand selector with shallow equality) keyed on
  [accounts, actor, events].
  - Drag listener re-attaches every render because xToAge is a fresh function.
  Flagged in DESIGN_NOTES; one useCallback away.
  - Hardcoded cash_reserves ID in engine for the inflation lookup. Should walk
  from the actor's cash_account_id instead.
  - Some duplicated logic in AccountInspector and EventInspector (NumericField
  is shared but the field-list construction isn't).
  - No memoization in the projection chart. Recharts handles its own diffing but
   the data-mapping happens fresh each render.
  - CSS is one big file with hardcoded colors instead of design tokens. Easy
  refactor when it matters.

  None of these require a rewrite. They're refactors within the existing
  structure.

  What would it take to turn this into a working app?

  Three phases. I'd estimate, but my track record on estimates is mediocre.

  Phase 0 — "real enough to use yourself" (small):
  - LocalStorage persistence (zustand persist middleware, ~20 lines).
  - JSON import/export of the whole store (one button, one file picker).
  - Error boundary at the top (so a single chart bug doesn't blank the page).
  - Engine memoization (the perf hot path).
  - Basic input validation in the inspector (no negative balances unless
  liability, ages within range, etc.).

  Phase 1 — "real enough to share":
  - Engine snapshot tests for the seed scenario. Pure function makes this cheap
  and load-bearing.
  - A handful of integration tests for create/edit/delete and the auto-event
  derivation.
  - Multi-scenario support (Scenario[] in store, one active). The
  compare-overlay view is genuinely as straightforward as you guessed.
  - Undo/redo via zustand middleware. The tool is exploratory; people will try
  things and want out.

  Phase 2 — "real enough to be useful for the user persona":
  - Bracket-based tax model. Federal + state tables, ordinary vs LTCG, IRMAA
  tiers. This is the largest single gap — the current effective-rate model
  literally cannot answer the questions the target user has. Touches engine,
  tooltip, summary strip.
  - RMDs (auto-event from tax-deferred when current_age >= 73).
  - Step-up basis at death (trivial primitive once you decide to add it).
  - Couples / joint with two ages and two SS streams.
  - Verb-first action editor or curated templates (you flagged this, I agree).

  Phase 3 — "real enough to charge for or distribute":
  - Cloud sync (optional, opt-in).
  - Mobile / responsive (or commit to desktop-only).
  - Telemetry, error reporting.
  - Polish: design tokens, accessibility audit, keyboard shortcuts.

  Will it lend itself to ongoing development?

  Yes, with one caveat.

  Yes because the architecture is additive. The most painful changes (engine
  schema, tree inheritance, event semantics) are settled; new features mostly
  mean new fields, new action types, new components. The pure engine means
  tax-model upgrades happen in one place. The discriminated-union selection
  means new selectable kinds (e.g., a "scenario" object) plug in cleanly.

  Caveat: the lack of tests will start to bite around Phase 1. Refactors will
  get scarier. Add tests early (Phase 0 if possible) — the engine especially
  deserves snapshot coverage before anyone touches it for taxes or RMDs.

  My recommendation

  Don't restart. A fresh build would lose six iterations of validated UX
  decisions, six iterations of validated schema decisions, and roughly two days
  of working code that already runs. The gaps are additive — not structural. The
   DESIGN_NOTES doc is comprehensive enough that a future implementer (or you,
  in six months) could pick this up cold.

  If you want, the highest-leverage 30-minute win right now is Phase 0's
  persistence + memoization, both of which I can do in this session — that takes
   the mock from "demo" to "I can actually use this for real planning over the
  weekend."

      444 +  const s = useStore.getState();                              
      445 +  const payload: ScenarioPayload = {                          
      446 +    schemaVersion: SCHEMA_VERSION,

      455 +export function importScenarioJson(json: string): { ok: boolea
          +n; reason?: string } {                                        
      456 +  let parsed: unknown;                                        
      457 +  try {                                                       
      458 +    parsed = JSON.parse(json);                                
      459 +  } catch (e) {                                               
      460 +    return { ok: false, reason: `not valid JSON: ${(e as Error
          +).message}` };                                                
      461 +  }                                                           
      462 +  if (!parsed || typeof parsed !== 'object') {                
      463 +    return { ok: false, reason: 'JSON root is not an object' }
          +;                                                             
      464 +  }                                                           
      465 +  const p = parsed as Partial<ScenarioPayload>;               
      466 +  if (!Array.isArray(p.accounts)) return { ok: false, reason: 
          +'missing or invalid `accounts` array' };                      
      467 +  if (!Array.isArray(p.events)) return { ok: false, reason: 'm
          +issing or invalid `events` array' };                          
      468 +  if (!p.actor || typeof p.actor !== 'object')                
      469 +    return { ok: false, reason: 'missing or invalid `actor` ob
          +ject' };                                                      
      470 +  if (p.schemaVersion !== undefined && p.schemaVersion > SCHEM
          +A_VERSION) {                                                  
      471 +    return {                                                  
      472 +      ok: false,                                              
      473 +      reason: `schema version ${p.schemaVersion} is newer than
          + this app supports (${SCHEMA_VERSION}).`,                     
      474 +    };                                                        
      475 +  }

          +ference stale ids.

       60  const newId = (prefix: string): string =>

       63 +// Sanity bounds at the store boundary. Keeps the engine from 
          +seeing                                                        
       64 +// nonsense states that the existing slider min/max already pr
          +event on                                                      
       65 +// the happy path, but that imports / direct mutations could o
          +therwise                                                      
       66 +// inject.                                                    
       67 +function clampAccount(a: AccountNode): AccountNode {          
       68 +  const next: AccountNode = { ...a };                         
       69 +  // Ages within a reasonable lifespan.                       
       70 +  if (next.start_age !== undefined)                           
       71 +    next.start_age = Math.max(0, Math.min(130, Math.round(next
          +.start_age)));                                                
       72 +  if (next.end_age !== undefined)                             
       73 +    next.end_age = Math.max(0, Math.min(130, Math.round(next.e
          +nd_age)));                                                    
       74 +  // end_age must not precede start_age.                      
       75 +  if (                                                        
       76 +    next.start_age !== undefined &&                           
       77 +    next.end_age !== undefined &&                             
       78 +    next.end_age < next.start_age                             
       79 +  )                                                           
       80 +    next.end_age = next.start_age;                            
       81 +  // Asset balances never go below zero (use a liability for d
          +ebt).                                                         
       82 +  if (next.kind === 'asset' && next.start_value !== undefined 
          +&& next.start_value < 0)                                      
       83 +    next.start_value = 0;                                     
       84 +  // Income / expense annual amounts are non-negative.        
       85 +  if (                                                        
       86 +    (next.kind === 'income' || next.kind === 'expense') &&    
       87 +    next.annual_amount !== undefined &&                       
       88 +    next.annual_amount < 0                                    
       89 +  )                                                           
       90 +    next.annual_amount = 0;                                   
       91 +  // Cost basis non-negative.                                 
       92 +  if (next.cost_basis !== undefined && next.cost_basis < 0) ne
          +xt.cost_basis = 0;                                            
       93 +  return next;                                                
       94 +}

      100 +    next.current_age + 1,                                     
      101 +    Math.min(130, Math.round(next.horizon_age)),              
      102 +  );                                                          
      103 +  return next;

  Available subcommands:

  - dev.sh check — typecheck (replaces the long nvm + tsc chain)
  - dev.sh verify — pings dev server, reports HTTP codes for key files
  - dev.sh tail — tails the most recent vite background log (best effort)
  - dev.sh dev — starts vite in foreground
  - dev.sh install — npm install with the right node

⏺ Phase 0 is in. Refresh http://localhost:5173/ to see it.

  What shipped:

  1. Engine memoization (store.ts): module-level caches for
  synthesizeAutoEvents, the merged events array, and the projection result. All
  three useProjection/useAllEvents callers share one cache. After the first
  component renders on a given state, the others hit cache. Slider drag now runs
   the engine once per state change, not three times.
  2. Error boundary at the app root (components/ErrorBoundary.tsx, wrapped in
  main.tsx). If any component throws, you get a fallback panel with the message
  + first 10 lines of the component stack, plus "Reload" and "Try to recover"
  buttons. (Note: needs CSS — visible but unstyled. I'll add tokens after.)
  3. localStorage persistence via zustand's persist middleware. Saves
  accounts/actor/events/dollarMode/expandedNodes under financial-modeler-v1 with
   schemaVersion: 1. Selection and hover are not persisted (ephemeral). The Set
  in expandedNodes is converted to an array on persist and back on rehydrate via
   a custom merge. Refresh the page now — your edits stick.
  4. JSON import/export (TopBar):
    - Export downloads <scenario_name>.json with schemaVersion, exportedAt
  timestamp, and the full scenario.
    - Import opens a file picker, confirms before replacing state, validates
  required keys, and rejects payloads with a future schema version.
  5. Input validation (clampAccount / clampActor): ages clamped to [0, 130],
  end_age >= start_age, asset start_value >= 0, income/expense annual_amount >= 
  0, cost_basis >= 0, horizon_age > current_age. Applied on every
  setAccountField and on every imported account/actor — so a bad JSON file can't
   poison the engine.

  Dev script (scripts/dev.sh) is in. Approve the permission rule once and I can
  use dev.sh check, dev.sh verify, dev.sh tail, dev.sh dev, dev.sh install
  without per-command prompts. Already used check and verify to confirm Phase 0
  is clean.

  Things to verify:
  - Edit something (move an event, change a balance), refresh the page → edits
  persist.
  - Click Export → .json downloads with your scenario.
  - Click Import on the downloaded file → replaces state with a confirmation
  prompt.
  - Open dev tools, throw a manual error in a component (or just trust the
  structure) → error boundary shows fallback.

  Known follow-ups before Phase 1 starts:
  - The error boundary fallback could use a few lines of CSS — currently it'll
  render but unstyled. 30 seconds of polish.
  - Storage key is financial-modeler-v1. If you want to wipe and start fresh,
  localStorage.removeItem('financial-modeler-v1') in dev tools.

  Ready to start Phase 1 (USER_GUIDE.md + USE_CASES.md + SCHEMA.md) when you
  are. Or if you want to road-test Phase 0 first by actually planning your
  retirement with it for a session, that's exactly the validation point Phase 0
  was built for.

⏺ Found it. Two bugs intertwined:

  1. Engine silently no-ops on unsupported fields. My add_value and set_value
  handlers only knew about start_value/balance (asset balances). Picking
  annual_amount from the action editor's dropdown produced a clean-looking
  config that the engine just ignored. No warning, no error — your −30% was a
  no-op against the expense.
  2. Defaults push you into the trap. When you create a "Shock / adjust" event,
  the scaffold pre-wires field: 'start_value'. If you then attach it to a
  stream, you have to remember to also flip the field to annual_amount. Easy to
  miss.

  Fixing the first one — adding annual_amount support to both action types —
  solves the immediate issue. The second is more of a UX nudge for later.

⏺ Phase 1 has three deliverables. Order matters — I'd start with USE_CASES.md
  because:

  1. Your retirement-planning session is the freshest source of truth I'll have
  for what real users actually try to do. Capturing those flows now (while you
  remember them) is higher value than writing them from imagination later.
  2. The use cases become the spine of Phase 2's integration tests, so the
  sooner they're written the sooner we can derive tests from them.
  3. USER_GUIDE.md will reference UCs (e.g., "see UC1 for a worked
  Roth-conversion example") so it's natural to write the UCs first.
  4. SCHEMA.md is mechanical — I can extract it from DESIGN_NOTES.md quickly any
   time.

  A tactical question for you before I write UCs: how concrete should the 
  expected outcomes be?

  Two options:

  - Concrete-numeric: "After applying UC1 to the seed scenario, terminal net
  worth at age 95 is $X.YM." Tightest possible test fixtures, but couples the
  doc to the seed and brittle to engine changes.
  - Directional: "Net worth at age 95 is higher than the no-conversion baseline
  by at least $X. Lifetime taxes are lower by at least $Y." More robust to
  engine evolution, and reads more like "what the user is trying to learn."

  I'd lean directional for the doc itself, with a separate, machine-readable
  fixture file (docs/fixtures/uc1.json-style) holding the exact expected numbers
   that Phase 2's snapshot tests bind to. Doc humans-readable, fixtures
  machine-readable, both updateable independently.

  Which way do you want to go? Then I'll write UC1–UC7 plus a UC for the bug you
   just found (shock on income/expense streams) plus a UC for "set up from CT
  scratch" mirroring your real session.

✻ Cogitated for 2m 59s

❯ I think that precise numeric outcomes are the realm of unit tests for the     
  engine. Fixture data should produce consistent outcomes. If we one day add    
  Monte Carlo simulations then the outcomes should be within a margin of error. 
   In other words, from initial model to year-over-year results should be       
  consistent.                                                                   
  For the end-to-end tests, we should focus on user interactions that produce   
  the expected model.                                                           
  How does that sound? It's not either option you suggested so I'd like your    
  thoughts.                                                                     

⏺ Better than either of mine. Let me reflect back what I think you're saying so
  we're aligned, then propose the format.

  The two-layer separation:

  ┌────────────────────┬──────────────────┬─────────────────────┬───────────┐
  │                    │                  │                     │ Doesn't   │
  │       Layer        │       Asks       │     Fails when      │  break    │
  │                    │                  │                     │   when    │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │                    │ "Given this      │ Math changes        │ UI        │
  │ Engine tests       │ model, what does │ (taxes, growth,     │ changes   │
  │                    │  it project?"    │ sequencing)         │           │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ E2E /              │ "Given these     │ UX changes          │           │
  │ user-interaction   │ user actions,    │ (selectors, store   │ Math      │
  │ tests              │ what model       │ mutators,           │ changes   │
  │                    │ results?"        │ validation rules)   │           │
  └────────────────────┴──────────────────┴─────────────────────┴───────────┘

  Each catches a different class of regression and they're repaired differently.
   A bracket-tax upgrade rewrites engine snapshots without touching E2E tests; a
   redesign of the action editor rewrites E2E tests without touching engine
  snapshots. That decoupling is the whole point.

  Engine tests are deterministic (today) and use exact numeric snapshots.
  When/if we add Monte Carlo, those become range assertions (toBeCloseTo,
  percentile bands) — but the input fixture → output relationship is still
  consistent enough that a regression looks like a regression, not noise.

  E2E tests assert on the resulting model state, not on the projection. Driving
  the UI to build a Roth ladder produces an events array containing a specific
  TimelineEvent. That's the test. The separate engine layer covers what that
  event does.

  Fixtures can be shared but assertions are separate. The seed is a fixture both
   layers can use. UC results can be dumped as fixtures the engine tests then
  snapshot. They share input data but don't share assertions — which is what you
   want.

  My proposed format for USE_CASES.md per use case:

  ## UC<n> — <name>

  **Narrative.** What the user is trying to figure out.

  **Starting state.** Seed, blank, or a specific fixture (e.g., "uc-3-output").

  **User actions.** Numbered, concrete, UI-level.
  1. Click "+ Add" → Ambient.
  2. Type "Connecticut" in the name field.
  3. Slide effective_tax_rate to 0.30.
  …

  **Expected resulting model.** Subset assertions on the store.
  - `accounts` contains a node with `name: "Connecticut"`, `kind: "ambient"`,
  `effective_tax_rate: 0.30`.
  - `actor.jurisdiction_account_id` references that node.
  - No other accounts/events touched.

  **Engine implications (informal — not asserted here).** What this *should*
  mean economically. Useful as a sanity-check sentence; engine tests handle the
  numbers.

  The "Expected resulting model" bullets are written in prose but should map
  cleanly to React Testing Library + zustand selector assertions:

  const ct = store.getState().accounts.find(a => a.name === 'Connecticut');
  expect(ct).toEqual(expect.objectContaining({ kind: 'ambient',
  effective_tax_rate: 0.30 }));
  expect(store.getState().actor.jurisdiction_account_id).toBe(ct.id);

  Two refinements I want to flag:

  1. One UC = one user goal, not one feature. "UC2: Move to Florida" walks the
  whole flow (create ambient, set rate, set as active OR create reparent event
  for future move) in service of answering the question "what does FL save me?"
  Multiple UI paths might satisfy a single UC, and the test asserts on outcome
  state, not on which path the user took. That keeps UCs aligned with user
  intent rather than UI mechanics.
  2. Drift between user-recommended path and code path. Sometimes there are
  multiple UI paths to the same state (e.g., reparent event vs. ActorInspector
  dropdown for changing jurisdiction). USE_CASES should pick the recommended
  path — the one we think is the cleanest UX — and that's what the E2E test
  exercises. Other paths get their own UCs if they're meaningfully different, or
   just get unit tests on the mutators.

  Sound right? If yes, I'll start with UC1 (Roth ladder), UC2 (Florida move via
  ActorInspector), UC3 (NUA on company stock), UC4 (market downturn shock to
  assets and to a stream — covers the bug we just fixed), UC5 (downsize house at
   75), UC6 (forced-sale gap years), UC7 (build from blank scenario à la your CT
   session). I'll skip the more speculative UCs until we walk these.

⏺ Not pedantic at all — you've spotted a real gap in my proposal. My UC1-7
  jumped straight to advanced tax-planning scenarios assuming the user already
  knows how to build a plan. The foundational ones are the actual onboarding
  ladder, and the more interesting question is "can a new user even get to the
  point of exploring Roth conversions?" If we don't write those first, we have
  no test that the basic flow works at all.

  Restructuring as a layered progression. Each layer's UCs assume the previous
  layer works:

  Layer 0 — Smoke / demo (no model creation)
  - UC1 Demo exploration. Load the app, the seed scenario is present and valid.
  Selecting any account or event updates the Inspector. Toggling Nominal ↔
  Today's $ updates both charts. Asserts on invariants: every entity is
  selectable, no broken references, charts render.

  Layer 1 — Build from scratch
  - UC2 Start a blank plan. Click New → Blank. Plan settings opens. Edit
  scenario name, current age, horizon age. Resulting model has the expected
  three-account scaffold (US Economy, cash, jurisdiction) and the actor pointing
   at them.
  - UC3 Set up your jurisdiction. From a blank plan, create your home state as
  an ambient with effective_tax_rate, set as active. Resulting model has
  actor.jurisdiction_account_id pointing at the new state.

  Layer 2 — Core accounts
  - UC4 Add a taxable brokerage holding. Create an asset, tag the custodian, set
   start_value and cost_basis. Resulting model contains the expected
  AccountNode.
  - UC5 Add a tax-deferred 401(k) and a Roth IRA. Two more assets with the right
   tax_treatment values.
  - UC6 Add a real-estate asset (house). Asset with asset_class: 'real_estate',
  cost_basis set.

  Layer 3 — Cashflow streams
  - UC7 Add a salary with an end date. Income stream with start_age, end_age,
  growth_rate. Auto-generated end-event appears in the timeline (✱).
  - UC8 Add Social Security starting later. Income stream with delayed start_age
   and a COLA growth_rate.
  - UC9 Add living expenses and healthcare. Two expense streams with realistic
  growth rates.

  Layer 4 — Major life events
  - UC10 Plan a market downturn. Add add_value event attached to all equity
  holdings, shock=−25%. Resulting event has the expected attached_account_ids
  and parameters.
  - UC11 Plan a Roth conversion ladder. Recurring transfer event from
  tax-deferred → Roth, age range, amount parameter. Resulting event has the
  expected shape.
  - UC12 Plan a state move. reparent event swapping jurisdiction at a future
  age. Resulting event has the expected new_parent.
  - UC13 Plan a house sale (downsize). liquidate event attached to the house at
  a future age. Resulting event has the expected target.
  - UC14 Belt-tightening shock to expenses. add_value with field: 
  'annual_amount' attached to an expense (the bug-regression case). Resulting
  event has the expected field value, and a separate engine test verifies the
  annual_amount actually drops.

  Layer 5 — Scenario exploration (pure read, no mutation)
  - UC15 Read the cone. Assert tooltip behavior on the net-worth chart
  (best/baseline/worst values for a hovered age).
  - UC16 Read cash-flow tooltip. Assert tooltip structure (Net at top,
  ordinary/LTCG breakdown).
  - UC17 Save and reload. Export JSON, clear state, import the JSON, model is
  restored.

  Deferred to later phases (mentioned but not written until the engine work
  lands):
  - Death and inheritance. Step-up basis at horizon is one primitive; full
  inheritance UI is Phase 4+.
  - RMD plans. Auto-events from tax_deferred accounts at age 73, requires the
  bracket-tax upgrade.
  - Couples / joint scenarios. Phase 4.

  That's 17 UCs. Honestly probably more than I'd write today — UC1 through UC9
  cover the foundation cleanly, UC10–14 cover the major events, UC15–17 are
  nice-to-have. I'd ship USE_CASES.md with UC1–UC14 written and UC15–17 listed 
  as stubs, and let the actual integration tests in Phase 2 drive any further
  additions.

  Sound good? I'll start writing UC1 and pace through. If a UC turns out to be
  unwritable (because the UX is awkward, or because the resulting model is
  ambiguous), I'll flag it as a UX gap to fix rather than write a contorted UC
  for it.

※ recap: Building a personal finance modeler; just finished Phase 0.5 polish plus a shock-on-expense engine fix and now starting Phase 1 docs. Next: write USE_CASES.md beginning with UC1 (demo exploration), pacing through UC1–UC14.

❯ Yes. I think you've touched on something very important about noticing        
  unwritable UCs. I think the same applies to untestable UCs. I think the UCs   
  will need to focus on application primitives until we have templates and      
  macros that make high level scenarios conceivable. Does that gibe with your   
  thinking?                                                                     

⏺ Yes — and you're sharpening something I had only gestured at. Let me name the
  distinction explicitly:

  Three levels of "use case":

  Level: Primitive
  Example: "Create a transfer event with target_account=X, parameters.amount=Y,
    attached to account Z"
  Testable now?: ✅
  Why: Resulting model state is unambiguous
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Level: Composition
  Example: "From a blank plan, add a CT jurisdiction, set it active, add a
    salary, add a Roth IRA"
  Testable now?: ✅
  Why: Sequence of primitives; resulting model still unambiguous
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Level: Strategy
  Example: "Plan a Roth conversion ladder optimized for my bracket placement"
  Testable now?: ❌ today
  Why: "What counts as a ladder?" "Does $50k/yr count?" "What ages?" — the
  answer
    requires interpretation, which currently lives in the user's head, not the
    schema

  Strategy-level UCs become testable when we have templates ("user invokes the
  Roth ladder template" → testable: did the template produce the expected
  events?). Until then, strategy lives in USER_GUIDE prose, not USE_CASES tests.

  Where I went wrong in my UC list: I named primitives by their strategic 
  interpretation — "UC11 Plan a Roth conversion ladder" rather than "Create a
  recurring transfer event." Same UC, same primitive, but the strategic framing
  leaks intent into the test name and obscures what's actually being asserted.
  Renaming:

  ┌────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │    Was (strategic)     │                 Is (primitive)                 │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Plan a Roth conversion │ Create a recurring transfer event              │
  │  ladder                │                                                │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Plan a state move      │ Create a reparent event or set                 │
  │                        │ actor.jurisdiction_account_id                  │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Plan a house sale      │ Create a liquidate event attached to a         │
  │                        │ real-estate asset                              │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Plan a market downturn │ Create an add_value event with                 │
  │                        │ field=start_value,                             │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Belt-tightening to     │ Create an add_value event with                 │
  │ expenses               │ field=annual_amount                            │
  └────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The mechanics each test are unchanged. The names now reflect what the
  assertion actually checks.

  Implication for the two docs:

  - USE_CASES.md — primitives + compositions of primitives. Spec for the
  integration test suite. Reads a bit dry; that's fine. Each UC is a testable
  mutation with a clear resulting model state.
  - USER_GUIDE.md — strategy-level prose. "To model a Roth conversion ladder, 
  you'll create a recurring transfer event going from a tax-deferred account to 
  a Roth account, repeated annually across your conversion years. See UC11 for 
  the exact UI sequence." This is where intent and interpretation live, with
  pointers down to the primitive UCs that demonstrate mechanics.

  When templates ship, this rebalances: a template invocation becomes a
  primitive, and what was strategy-level prose can collapse into a UC of the
  form "user invokes the X template, resulting model contains the expected
  events." That's why your point about "until we have templates" is the right
  boundary — it's exactly when the strategy-level becomes testable.

  One concession to your principle worth flagging: UC1 (demo exploration) and
  UC17 (save/load round-trip) are invariant tests rather than mutation tests.
  They don't fit "user action → resulting model" cleanly because they assert on
  shape and stability, not on a transition. I think they still belong in
  USE_CASES but tagged as invariant UCs. Alternative: they're separate from
  USE_CASES and live in a smoke-tests doc. I lean toward keeping them in
  USE_CASES with a tag, because anyone reading "the things this app must
  support" wants those covered too.

  Sound right? If yes, I'll start writing USE_CASES.md with the renamed
  primitive list.

  Things that emerged while writing — worth surfacing:

  1. Two paths to set jurisdiction (UC6, paths A and B) didn't fit a single
  primitive UC because they're genuinely different mutations: path A is
  setActorField('jurisdiction_account_id', ...) triggered from the
  AccountInspector banner; path B is the same mutation triggered from the
  ActorInspector dropdown. Both produce the same model state. I documented both
  as variants of the same UC because the resulting model is identical — they're
  alternative primitive paths to the same primitive operation, and tests can run
   both.
  2. UC22 (multi-action events) is the only "composition of compositions" in the
   doc. It asserts that actions is a list, ordered, applied in sequence. Worth
  flagging because today's seed has zero multi-action events — every seed event
  has exactly one action. If we never compose actions in seed scenarios, this UC
   could go untested in practice. Decision: write it anyway; the test exercises
  the primitive even if the seed doesn't.
  3. UC11's "auto-event isn't in events array" assertion is load-bearing for
  Phase 4. When RMD auto-events arrive, the same derivation pattern applies:
  synthesizer adds them at projection time, they show up in useAllEvents() but
  not in useStore().events. Locking this in as a test now means RMD additions
  will inherit the same behavior with zero new test code.
  4. One untestable thing I noticed while writing: there's no UC for "the
  scenario name in the topbar updates when actor.scenario_name changes." That's
  a sub-test of UC4, but it tests a render binding rather than a model mutation.
   Per our principle, render bindings live in component-level tests, not UCs. I
  left it out.

  Now USER_GUIDE.md (task-oriented prose, references UCs by number) and
  SCHEMA.md (mechanical extraction from DESIGN_NOTES). Want me to keep going
  through Phase 1, or pause here for you to read through USE_CASES?

  Things that emerged while writing — worth flagging:

  1. The "moving to a tax-friendlier state" section had to distinguish two cases
   (initial residence vs future move). One uses the ActorInspector dropdown, the
   other uses a reparent event. The naming-by-primitive principle from earlier
  helped here — the doc is honest that these are two different primitives even
  though strategically they're "where I live." I also link to UC6 (initial) and
  UC20 (future move) so the reader can find the exact sequence for either.
  2. The NUA section was the trickiest to keep honest. NUA in the real world is
  one of the more complex tax provisions, and the current model approximates it
  via the existing liquidate action's special-casing of tax_deferred + 
  cost_basis. I described the worked example by pointing at the seed's "Company
  stock (NUA candidate)" rather than walking the user through composing it from
  primitives — until the action editor learns NUA-aware defaults, the seed
  example is the easiest path.
  3. The "What's not modeled yet" section lists every Phase 4 deferral with
  explicit pointers to the roadmap. This is load-bearing for trust: a user who
  notices "this tool is using a single effective tax rate" should immediately
  see that this is known and on the path, not a hidden flaw. Same for RMDs.
  4. One glossary entry I'm slightly uneasy about: "Roth conversion ladder." I
  called it "a pattern, not a primitive." That's accurate today, but if Phase 4
  adds it as a template, the glossary will become wrong. Easy fix when that
  ships; flagging here so we remember to revisit.

  DESIGN_NOTES.md. Want me to keep going, or pause here for you to read
  USE_CASES.md + USER_GUIDE.md first?

  One thing worth flagging from writing SCHEMA.md:

  The reference table for add_value and set_value field handling makes a current
   limitation glaringly visible: when field is anything other than
  start_value/balance/annual_amount, the action is a silent no-op. This is the
  bug class you hit with the market-downturn-on-expenses case. The fix wasn't
  just to support annual_amount — it was a hint that every unsupported field is
  a footgun.

  For Phase 2 testing, this matters: UC18/UC19 lock in the working cases, but a
  robustness UC like "configuring an action with an unsupported field warns the
  user instead of silently accepting it" would be reasonable to add. I didn't
  write that UC because it requires a UX decision (warn? disable the option?
  validate at edit time?). Worth raising when we get to test implementation.

  with a pointer to the new docs, or skip and just commit?
